4 2𝑑supersymmetric field theories
Guide to this section. Sections 1 and 2 fixed the algebra and the common words, and Section 3
built the four-supercharge holomorphic world of 4𝑑N = 1 field theory with its extremization
principle. This section builds the two-dimensional world, as a subject to be owned rather than
a list to be surveyed. Two dimensions is where supersymmetry splits by worldsheet chirality,
and that split is the source of everything special here. The section is two complete worlds and a
shared interface. Block A is the balanced four-supercharge N = (2, 2) world: two holomorphic
sectors (𝑊and e
𝑊), the gauged linear sigma model as dynamics, and the A/B twists with mirror
symmetry. Block B is the chiral two-supercharge N = (0, 2) world: Fermi multiplets with
independent 𝐸/𝐽data, holomorphic bundles, gauge and gravitational anomalies, 𝑐-extremization,
and triality. Block C is the shared interface: the elliptic genus and a comparison table. Each
structure is developed the same way: why it has to appear, its precise definition, one canonical
calculation done in full, what goes wrong if it is misused. The deep theorems (the phase, triality,
𝑐-extremization, and elliptic-genus modularity theorems) are stated and cited; the working
calculations are done here. Where the argument needs the chiral ring, the trial R-symmetry, or
the extremization principle, it recalls Section 3 rather than re-deriving it.
What this section delivers
The 2𝑑SUSY kinematics: the (𝑝, 𝑞) count, worldsheet chirality, N = (2, 2) versus N = (0, 2),
and the R-symmetries 𝑈(1)𝑉, 𝑈(1)𝐴(§4.1). Block A, the N = (2, 2) world: the two holomorphic
sectors 𝑊versus e
𝑊(§4.2); the gauged linear sigma model, the CP𝑁−1 quantum cohomology
C[Σ]/(Σ𝑁−𝑞), and the quintic phases (§4.3); the A/B twists and the Hori–Vafa mirror (§4.4).
Block B, the N = (0, 2) world: the 𝐸/𝐽scalar potential and closure Í
𝑎Tr(𝐸𝑎𝐽𝑎) = 0 (§4.5);
bundles and the gauged N = (2, 2) →N = (0, 2) 𝐸-term (§4.6); anomalies and 𝑐-extremization to
𝑐𝑅= 3𝑘/(𝑘+ 2) (§4.7); and triality (§4.8). Block C, the shared interface: the elliptic genus (§4.9)
and the N = (2, 2)-versus-N = (0, 2) comparison (§4.10).

## Page 69


4.1 2𝑑kinematics and R-symmetries
Everything special about two dimensions starts from one fact: a spinor in two dimensions can
be Majorana–Weyl. A Majorana–Weyl spinor carries a single real component and a definite
worldsheet chirality, left-moving or right-moving. A supercharge inherits that chirality, and so the
amount of supersymmetry cannot be quoted by a single number as in four dimensions. It is a pair
(𝑝, 𝑞) with 𝑝left-moving and 𝑞right-moving real supercharges, and the total count is the sum
# supercharges = 𝑝+ 𝑞,
(4.1)
the 2𝑑Majorana–Weyl reality row of Section 1.
This section treats the two ends of the
four-supercharge family. The maximal case is N = (2, 2),
N = (2, 2) :
𝑝= 2, 𝑞= 2,
# supercharges = 4,
(4.2)
the dimensional reduction of 4𝑑N = 1 on a two-torus: the four real supercharges survive, now
sorted into two right-moving and two left-moving,
4𝑑N = 1
𝑇2
−−−→2𝑑N = (2, 2),
4 = 2 + 2,
(4.3)
the four-supercharge row that reads 4𝑑N = 1 ≡3𝑑N = 2 ≡2𝑑N = (2, 2) on the Section 1
ladder. The minimal case is N = (0, 2),
N = (0, 2) :
𝑝= 0, 𝑞= 2,
# supercharges = 2,
(4.4)
two real supercharges, all right-moving. This is the chiral, holomorphic half of N = (2, 2): it
keeps the two right-moving supercharges and discards the two left-moving ones. It has no four-
dimensional Lorentz-invariant parent, since there is no way to sort four-dimensional supercharges
into right-movers and left-movers, so its structures are the genuinely two-dimensional part of the
section.
The section map. The split by chirality organizes the section. Block A is the N = (2, 2) world,
four balanced supercharges, Kähler and complex geometry: the two holomorphic sectors (§4.2),
the gauged linear sigma model (§4.3), the A/B twists and mirror symmetry (§4.4). Block B is
the N = (0, 2) world, the chiral half with two right-movers and no left-movers, a holomorphic
bundle over a target: 𝐸/𝐽data (§4.5), bundles and the gauged 𝐸-term (§4.6), anomalies and
𝑐-extremization (§4.7), triality (§4.8). Block C is the shared interface: the elliptic genus (§4.9), a
N = (0, 2) object that specializes to N = (2, 2), and a comparison table (§4.10). Each structure
sits in the world it belongs to: mirror symmetry and the twists are N = (2, 2)-only, triality and
𝑐-extremization are N = (0, 2)-central, the elliptic genus is genuinely shared.
The two R-symmetries. Why should two dimensions carry two abelian R-symmetries where
four dimensions carries one? Because the two right-moving and two left-moving supercharges
can be rotated by two commuting phases, not one. A N = (2, 2) theory has an R-symmetry
group that contains two commuting 𝑈(1) factors, the vector R-symmetry 𝑈(1)𝑉and the axial
R-symmetry 𝑈(1)𝐴. The vector R-symmetry acts with the same sign on the left- and right-moving
supercharges; the axial R-symmetry acts with opposite signs. Writing the two supercharge phases
as 𝑄± →𝑒𝑖𝛼±𝑄± (the subscript is the worldsheet chirality), the two combinations are
𝑈(1)𝑉: 𝛼+ = 𝛼−= 𝛼𝑉,
𝑈(1)𝐴: 𝛼+ = −𝛼−= 𝛼𝐴,
(4.5)

## Page 70


the vector rotation the diagonal, the axial the anti-diagonal. This is not bookkeeping. The two
R-symmetries distinguish the two topological twists of §4.4 (A uses 𝑈(1)𝑉, B uses 𝑈(1)𝐴), and
the axial one is the fragile factor that can be anomalous. Its anomaly is proportional to the first
Chern class of a sigma-model target, so 𝑈(1)𝐴survives precisely on a Calabi–Yau, which is why
the B-twist (and only the B-twist) is gated by the Calabi–Yau condition, and why the sigma-model
coupling can run when only 𝑈(1)𝑉survives.
The two sectors carry opposite axial charge. Under 𝑈(1)𝑉a chiral Φ and a twisted-chiral Σ
both carry their vector R-charge, and 𝑊(Φ), e
𝑊(Σ) must each be 𝑈(1)𝑉-neutral as a superspace
integral. Under 𝑈(1)𝐴the chiral and twisted-chiral fields carry opposite sign, because the axial
rotation distinguishes 𝐷+ from 𝐷−. So the two rings of §4.2 are graded by different R-symmetries,
the algebraic root of mirror symmetry, which exchanges the two R-symmetries and so the two
sectors,
𝑈(1)𝑉↔𝑈(1)𝐴,
Φ ↔Σ,
𝑊↔e
𝑊.
(4.6)
Carry away one fact: two dimensions has two abelian R-symmetries because it has two
holomorphic sectors, and each sector is graded by the R-symmetry its superspace measure
respects.
The multiplet vocabulary. The multiplets are graded by which combinations of the supersym-
metry covariant derivatives annihilate them. Three multiplets carry the N = (2, 2) content.
• Chiral Φ, annihilated by both barred derivatives ¯𝐷±Φ = 0. Content: a complex scalar 𝜙,
a Dirac fermion (one right-mover 𝜓+ and one left-mover 𝜓−), and an auxiliary field. It
is the reduction of the 4𝑑N = 1 chiral multiplet of Section 2, and it carries the ordinary
superpotential 𝑊(Φ).
• Twisted-chiral Σ, annihilated by the opposite pair ¯𝐷+Σ = 𝐷−Σ = 0. It has the same field
content as a chiral (a complex scalar, a Dirac fermion, an auxiliary) and the opposite
constraint. It has no four-dimensional analog. It is indispensable because the field strength
of a N = (2, 2) vector, Σ = ¯𝐷+𝐷−𝑉, is twisted-chiral, and its holomorphic self-coupling
is the twisted superpotential e
𝑊(Σ), developed in §4.2.
• Vector 𝑉, carrying the gauge field, a Dirac gaugino, and the auxiliary 𝐷-field. In two
dimensions the gauge field has no propagating polarization, so the physical content of the
vector sits in its twisted-chiral field strength Σ.
The two chiralities are the two constraints
chiral :
¯𝐷±Φ = 0,
twisted-chiral :
¯𝐷+Σ = 𝐷−Σ = 0,
Σ = ¯𝐷+𝐷−𝑉,
(4.7)
the opposite pairing ¯𝐷+, 𝐷−marking the twisted sector. The N = (0, 2) algebra has one barred
right-moving derivative ¯𝐷+, and its three multiplets are graded by worldsheet chirality.
• Chiral Φ, annihilated by ¯𝐷+. Content: a complex scalar 𝜙and a single right-moving Weyl
fermion 𝜓+, nothing left-moving.
• Fermi Λ, the genuinely new object. Content: a single left-moving Weyl fermion 𝜆−and no
propagating scalar. It is only constrained chiral, ¯𝐷+Λ = 𝐸, with 𝐸a holomorphic function
of the chiral fields; 𝐸= 0 is a free Fermi multiplet, and a nonzero 𝐸is one of the two
pieces of N = (0, 2) interaction data of §4.5.
• Vector 𝑉, carrying the gauge field and a left-moving gaugino.
70


## Page 71


The two chiral N = (0, 2) constraints are
chiral :
¯𝐷+Φ = 0,
Fermi :
¯𝐷+Λ = 𝐸(Φ),
(4.8)
the Fermi only constrained chiral, its right-hand side 𝐸the first piece of N = (0, 2) interaction
data. The Fermi multiplet is what makes two dimensions chiral: it supplies a left-moving fermion
with no bosonic partner, so a generic N = (0, 2) spectrum has a right-minus-left imbalance with
no analog in four dimensions.
The N = (2, 2) →N = (0, 2) decomposition. Every N = (2, 2) multiplet is a sum of
N = (0, 2) multiplets, and the bookkeeping is a clean first check. Count the content of a
N = (2, 2) chiral: one complex scalar (two real bosonic fields), one right-moving fermion, and
one left-moving fermion, written (boson, right, left) = (2, 1, 1). A N = (0, 2) chiral has content
(2, 1, 0): the scalar and the right-mover, no left-mover. A N = (0, 2) Fermi has content (0, 0, 1):
the missing left-mover, nothing else. So
N = (2, 2) chiral = N = (0, 2) chiral + N = (0, 2) Fermi,
(2, 1, 1) = (2, 1, 0) + (0, 0, 1),
(4.9)
component by component. The Fermi is not optional in the decomposition: dropping it would
leave the N = (2, 2) chiral without its left-moving fermion, breaking the count. Likewise a
N = (2, 2) vector splits as
N = (2, 2) vector = N = (0, 2) vector + N = (0, 2) adjoint chiral,
(4.10)
the N = (0, 2) vector carrying the gauge field and the left-moving gaugino, the adjoint N = (0, 2)
chiral carrying the complex scalar and right-moving fermion. This is how any N = (2, 2)
theory is read as a N = (0, 2) theory, and it is exactly why the anomaly cancellations of §4.7
are automatic for N = (2, 2) theories: each N = (2, 2) chiral brings a N = (0, 2) chiral and a
N = (0, 2) Fermi in matched pairs, so their chiral contributions cancel term by term. Table 4.1
collects the content.
Common misconception: N = (0, 2) is “the N = 2 of two dimensions”
The pair N = (0, 2) names two supercharges, not four. Reading it as “N = 2” by analogy with
four dimensions, and so expecting four supercharges, confuses it with N = (2, 2), which is the
genuine four-supercharge algebra. The two indices are the left-moving and right-moving counts, so
N = (0, 2) is two right-movers and zero left-movers; the total is 𝑝+ 𝑞= 2. Likewise (4, 4), with
eight supercharges, is a different and larger algebra, outside this section. The label counts the two
chiralities separately precisely because 2𝑑supersymmetry is graded by worldsheet chirality.
Block A. The N = (2, 2) world
The next three sections build N = (2, 2) dynamics as one coherent world: its two holomorphic
sectors and rings (§4.2), the gauged linear sigma model that turns those data into Calabi–Yau
geometry (§4.3), and the two topological twists with mirror symmetry that compute the two
rings and exchange them (§4.4). All of it is N = (2, 2)-specific; the N = (0, 2) world of Block
B keeps only the pieces that survive discarding two supercharges.

4.2 The N = (2, 2) holomorphic sectors and their rings
A 4𝑑N = 1 theory has one holomorphic self-coupling, the superpotential. A 2𝑑N = (2, 2)
theory has two, and understanding why is the first genuinely two-dimensional lesson. The reason
is the twisted-chiral multiplet: because the two right-moving and two left-moving supercharges
combine in two inequivalent ways, there are two kinds of holomorphic constraint a multiplet can
satisfy, and each supports its own superspace integral.

4.3 The gauged linear sigma model, from CP𝑁−1 to the quintic
The workhorse construction of 2𝑑N = (2, 2) theories is the gauged linear sigma model. It is an
abelian (or product-unitary) gauge theory whose low-energy physics computes geometry: in one
regime it is a nonlinear sigma model on a Calabi–Yau, in another a Landau–Ginzburg orbifold,
and the quantum cohomology of a projective target falls out of a single one-loop calculation
on its Coulomb branch. We lead with the simplest such calculation, CP𝑁−1, because it teaches
twisted-chiral fields, the FI/theta parameter, quantum cohomology, the vacuum count, and the
A-model at once; then the quintic as the flagship Calabi–Yau.

D-term sets the moment map of the gauge action equal to the FI parameter,

Common misconception: the effective twisted superpotential sign is +𝑁Σ(log Σ −1)
The one-loop term in (4.23) carries a minus sign, −𝑁Σ(log Σ −1). This sign is not a convention one
is free to flip: it is fixed by the requirement that the vacuum equation give Σ𝑁= 𝑒−𝑡= 𝑞with 𝑞the
instanton factor. The opposite sign +𝑁Σ(log Σ −1) would give Σ𝑁= 𝑒+𝑡, inconsistent both with
the instanton weight 𝑞= 𝑒−𝑡and with the Hori–Vafa mirror of §4.4. The calculation that backs this
section carries the sign that reproduces Σ𝑁= 𝑒−𝑡; a draft that writes the plus sign is computing the
wrong vacuum equation.
The two phases and one RG story. Now add a superpotential and let the target be Calabi–Yau,
so the gauged linear sigma model flows to a conformal field theory. The low-energy physics
depends on the FI chamber, and the two chambers realize two very different-looking descriptions
of the same infrared theory. For 𝑟≫0 the D-term forces some positively-charged field nonzero,
the origin is excluded, and setting the D-term to 𝑟and dividing by the gauge group is the
symplectic reduction

## Page 76


equivalently the geometric-invariant-theory (GIT) quotient, a toric variety whose fan is read from
the charge matrix. The F-terms of 𝑊then cut a hypersurface (or complete intersection) inside
𝑋𝑟: this is the geometric phase, a nonlinear sigma model on a Calabi–Yau. For 𝑟≪0 the same
D-term forces a negatively-charged field nonzero instead, Higgsing the gauge group to a finite
subgroup and leaving the remaining fields as a Landau–Ginzburg orbifold with superpotential 𝑊:
the Landau–Ginzburg phase.
The prose must be careful: the naive picture (“two chambers of the 𝑟-axis, smoothly
connected except at one point”) is only the classical cartoon. The precise statement has four
layers. Classically the sign of 𝑟divides the FI axis into chambers. The FI and theta parameters
then complexify into 𝑡= 𝑟−𝑖𝜃, ranging over a cylinder, the quantum Kähler moduli space
whose two ends are the classical chambers. That moduli space carries a discriminant locus,
the codimension-one set where the theory becomes singular (a noncompact Coulomb branch
opens and a new massless state appears); it is one point for one-parameter models but a genuine
subvariety in general. Finally, the physical statement: the gauged linear sigma model is one
theory flowing to one infrared superconformal field theory in the Calabi–Yau case, and the
geometric and Landau–Ginzburg descriptions are two ultraviolet regimes of that one flow. They
are not two unrelated theories, but neither does the classical 𝑟-axis by itself prove a globally
smooth interpolation, which is a statement about the quantum moduli space and its discriminant.
The full analysis (secondary fan, discriminant, analytic continuation) is the phase theorem, not
developed here; the worked computation here is the quintic (Figure 4.1).
The discriminant point makes “two regimes of one flow” precise. On the Coulomb branch
(large Σ) the effective twisted superpotential of a Calabi–Yau gauged linear sigma model
generically has no critical point at finite Σ: the D-term lifts it. At a special 𝑡on the discriminant
a would-be massive vacuum becomes massless, a critical point runs off to Σ →∞, and a
new massless degree of freedom appears in the infrared. That is the singular locus: not a
thermodynamic phase boundary but a point where the low-energy theory degenerates because
a massless state (a noncompact Coulomb branch) appears. A generic theta angle steers the
straight-line path in 𝑡around it, so the two descriptions are connected by a path along which
the theory never degenerates. This is “no phase transition”: the connection runs through the
quantum moduli space, avoiding the discriminant, a statement about where the theory is singular,
not about the sign of classical 𝑟.
The Calabi–Yau condition on the geometric phase is a condition on the charges. The axial
R-symmetry 𝑈(1)𝐴is non-anomalous, equivalently the target has vanishing first Chern class,
precisely when

(the target is Fano or of general type) and it is not the conformal phase this section exemplifies.

Common misconception: the two phases are different theories
The geometric (𝑟≫0) and Landau–Ginzburg (𝑟≪0) phases are two ultraviolet regimes of one
gauged linear sigma model, flowing to one infrared superconformal field theory in the Calabi–Yau case,
not distinct theories. The geometric phase is the GIT (symplectic) quotient {Í
𝑖𝑄𝑎

(a toric variety), further cut by the F-terms of 𝑊, not the naive vanishing locus of 𝑊alone (which
would give the wrong dimension). The interpolation between the two ends is smooth in the quantum
Kähler moduli space (the 𝑡= 𝑟−𝑖𝜃cylinder), away from its discriminant locus (where a noncompact
Coulomb branch opens and a massless state appears); do not read the classical 𝑟-axis by itself as
proving a globally smooth interpolation. And the phases exist for any FI chamber: Í
𝑎𝑄𝑎= 0 is
the Calabi–Yau, conformal, anomaly-free condition of the intended example, not a prerequisite for a
phase to exist.
4.4 The A and B topological twists and mirror symmetry
A N = (2, 2) theory admits two inequivalent topological twists, and they compute the two rings
of §4.2. This is N = (2, 2)-specific technology, placed here immediately after the gauged linear
sigma model whose rings it computes, not among the shared tools of Block C. The twist mixes
the Lorentz rotation with an R-symmetry, turning one supercharge into a scalar nilpotent operator
whose cohomology is the topological ring. Which R-symmetry is used distinguishes the two
models, the 𝑈(1)𝑉versus 𝑈(1)𝐴distinction of §4.1.
78


## Page 79

A-model localizes on holomorphic maps to the target (the worldsheet instantons) and computes
the quantum Kähler data: Gromov–Witten invariants and the quantum-cohomology ring. For
CP𝑁−1 it is exactly the ring of §4.3,

Mirror symmetry and the Hori–Vafa construction
Mirror symmetry is a duality between two full N = (2, 2) theories, not a property of their
twists. A N = (2, 2) sigma model on 𝑋is physically equivalent, as an untwisted theory, to a
N = (2, 2) theory on a mirror ˆ𝑋; for a gauged linear sigma model the mirror is the Hori–Vafa
dual Landau–Ginzburg / Toda theory, and needs no twist. The construction is at field level:
dualize each charged chiral Φ𝑖to a periodic twisted-chiral 𝑌𝑖, real part |𝜙𝑖|2, shift symmetry the
phase of Φ𝑖. The dual is a Landau–Ginzburg model of the 𝑌𝑖with mirror twisted superpotential

to the two rings, and gates the B-twist by the Calabi–Yau condition.
Block B. The N = (0, 2) world
The next four sections build N = (0, 2) dynamics as the second coherent world. Discarding the
two left-moving supercharges of N = (2, 2) frees the left-moving fermions from their partners,
and the consequences run through the whole part: two independent holomorphic data (𝐸, 𝐽) per
Fermi and a scalar potential built from them (§4.5); a holomorphic bundle over the target, seen
sharply as a gauged N = (2, 2) →N = (0, 2) deformation (§4.6); a gauge anomaly that must
cancel, a gravitational anomaly that only matches, and the 𝑐-extremization principle (§4.7); and
an order-three triality (§4.8).
4.5 N = (0, 2) Fermi multiplets, the E/J data, and the scalar potential

Fermi multiplet. Why those data are not free, and what they do to the scalar potential, is the
central physics of the N = (0, 2) world. We build it as field theory first, then read off the standard
fixture.

4.6 N = (0, 2) bundles and deformations of N = (2, 2) GLSMs
The most important reason N = (0, 2) theories matter, beyond quiver technology, is geometric.
A N = (2, 2) nonlinear sigma model on a Kähler target 𝑋has both its fermions valued in the
tangent bundle 𝑇𝑋, the left-movers locked to the right-movers by extended supersymmetry. A
N = (0, 2) model relaxes that locking: the right-movers still couple to 𝑇𝑋, but the left-moving
Fermi fermions couple to an independent holomorphic vector bundle 𝐸→𝑋,
N = (2, 2) NLSM : 𝜓± ∈𝑇𝑋,
N = (0, 2) NLSM : 𝜓+ ∈𝑇𝑋, 𝜆−∈𝐸,
(4.61)
and the N = (2, 2) theory is recovered as the special case 𝐸= 𝑇𝑋. The freedom to choose
𝐸≠𝑇𝑋is what makes N = (0, 2) models a genuinely larger world (heterotic compactifications,
bundle-deformed Calabi–Yau models). But 𝐸is not arbitrary: consistency of the quantum theory
demands anomaly conditions, the geometric avatar of the 2𝑑gauge and gravitational anomalies
of the next section,
ch2(𝐸) = ch2(𝑇𝑋),
𝑐1(𝐸) ≡𝑐1(𝑇𝑋)
(mod 2).
(4.62)
The first is the vanishing of the sigma-model gauge anomaly (the left-moving fermion one-loop
trace matches the right-moving one, degree by degree); the second is the Green–Schwarz
condition on the first Chern class. They are the target image of the chiral fermion traces that §4.7
treats as the 2𝑑gauge and gravitational anomalies, so the abstract 𝐸/𝐽data are the local shadow
of a bundle over a geometry.

data by extended supersymmetry, but how they lock depends on whether the theory is gauged.

Worked instance: a N = (0, 2) deformation of the quintic GLSM
The freedom to choose 𝐸≠𝑇𝑋is best seen as a deformation of the N = (2, 2) point just worked.
At the N = (2, 2) (tangent-bundle) locus the Fermi data are the locked pair (4.66). A N = (0, 2)
deformation replaces the locked 𝐽𝑖by more general holomorphic functions 𝐽𝑖= 𝐽𝑖(𝜙, 𝑃) and
deforms the 𝐸𝑖away from (4.65), subject to two constraints: the closure Í
𝑎Tr(𝐸𝑎𝐽𝑎) = 0
of (4.54) (supersymmetry) and the bundle-anomaly conditions (4.62). The deformed model
describes the same Calabi–Yau 𝑋but with the left-moving fermions coupled to a bundle 𝐸≠𝑇𝑋:
a bundle-deformed gauged linear sigma model. The anomaly conditions bound the family,
keeping ch2(𝐸) = ch2(𝑇𝑋) and 𝑐1(𝐸) ≡𝑐1(𝑇𝑋) mod 2, so the surviving deformations are those
preserving the two characteristic classes.
The lesson is the shape of the moduli space. The N = (2, 2) locus (complex-structure and
Kähler deformations) sits inside a larger N = (0, 2) moduli space (bundle deformations with
𝐸≠𝑇𝑋), the enlargement the extra freedom in 𝐸and 𝐽that the anomaly conditions do not kill.
So N = (0, 2) models are the field-theory description of heterotic compactifications on (𝑋, 𝐸),
their marginal deformations the bundle moduli. Which deformations survive is controlled here
by (4.62) together with the closure (4.54).
4.7 2d anomalies and c-extremization
Chirality makes two dimensions dangerous, and the danger is quantified by chiral anomalies of
two kinds, handled in opposite ways. It is also chirality that lets an extremization principle fix
the exact superconformal R-symmetry and the central charges. We take the anomalies first, then
𝑐-extremization, which is built from them.
The gauge anomaly. A 2𝑑chiral fermion in a gauge representation contributes to the gauge
two-point anomaly with a sign set by its worldsheet chirality: right-movers one sign, left-movers
the other. Collecting the N = (0, 2) content, the right-moving fermions in chiral multiplets
enter with a plus, and the left-moving fermions in Fermi multiplets and the gaugino (an adjoint
left-mover) enter with a minus. The theory is consistent only if the total vanishes,
Trchiral𝑇𝐴𝑇𝐵−TrFermi𝑇𝐴𝑇𝐵−Tradj𝑇𝐴𝑇𝐵= 0.
(4.70)
On the C4 brick, where the content per node is four adjoint chirals, three adjoint Fermis, and one
adjoint gaugino, this reads in units of the adjoint index
4𝑇(adj) −3𝑇(adj) −𝑇(adj) = 0,
(4.71)
the clean 4 −3 −1 = 0. The gaugino term is load-bearing: dropping it leaves 4 −3 = 1 ≠0,
an inconsistent theory. For a N = (2, 2) theory the cancellation is automatic, and (4.9) is why:
each N = (2, 2) chiral supplies one N = (0, 2) chiral and one N = (0, 2) Fermi in the same
representation, so their traces cancel. For 𝑛N = (2, 2) chirals plus one N = (2, 2) vector, the
N = (0, 2) counts are 𝑛+1 chirals (the 𝑛matter chirals plus the vector’s adjoint chiral), 𝑛Fermis,
and one vector, so (4.70) reads
(𝑛+ 1) −𝑛−1 = 0
(4.72)

The gravitational anomaly. The gravitational anomaly is the net worldsheet chirality of the
massless fermions,
𝑐𝑅−𝑐𝐿= Tr 𝛾3,
(4.75)
where 𝛾3 = +1 on a right-mover and −1 on a left-mover, so Tr 𝛾3 counts right-movers minus
left-movers. Unlike the gauge anomaly, this is generically nonzero, and a nonzero value is allowed
and physical. It is a ’t Hooft anomaly of the 2𝑑Lorentz (chirality) symmetry, invariant along
the renormalization-group flow, and it must match between two descriptions of the same theory
rather than cancel. A content of three right-movers and one left-mover has 𝑐𝑅−𝑐𝐿= 3 −1 = 2, a
consistent chiral theory. The C4/Z4 brick, with 16 right-moving chiral fermions, 12 left-moving
Fermi fermions, and 4 left-moving gauginos, has
𝑐𝑅−𝑐𝐿= 16 −12 −4 = 0,
(4.76)
a non-chiral theory. The matching statement is the working tool: two descriptions of the same
infrared physics must report the same 𝑐𝑅−𝑐𝐿, one of the invariants the three triality frames
share. As a matching example, two spectra grouped differently but sharing the net chirality,
A:
4 right + 2 left :
𝑐𝑅−𝑐𝐿= 4 −2 = 2,
B:
(3 + 1) right + 2 left :
𝑐𝑅−𝑐𝐿= (3 + 1) −2 = 2,
(4.77)
report the same 𝑐𝑅−𝑐𝐿= 2: the anomaly sees only the net right-minus-left count, and being
RG-invariant it is the same in the ultraviolet and infrared. This is what makes it a diagnostic: if
two ultraviolet Lagrangians claiming the same infrared theory report different 𝑐𝑅−𝑐𝐿, one is
wrong, and the computation is a one-line count of fermions weighted by 𝛾3.
Common misconception: the gravitational anomaly must cancel
Two dimensions has two distinct chiral-anomaly conditions, handled differently. The gauge anomaly
Trchiral −TrFermi −Tradj = 0 must cancel for the theory to exist.
The gravitational anomaly
𝑐𝑅−𝑐𝐿= Tr 𝛾3, the net fermion chirality, is generically nonzero; it is a ’t Hooft anomaly of the
2𝑑Lorentz symmetry, RG-invariant, and it must match between descriptions (for instance the three
triality frames), not cancel. A nonzero 𝑐𝑅−𝑐𝐿is allowed and physical; an uncancelled gauge anomaly
is fatal.

The saddle-versus-maximum split is structural, not accidental.
Four-dimensional 𝑎-
maximization extremizes a cubic anomaly Tr 𝑅3 whose Hessian quadratic form is negative
definite, so 𝑎is truly maximized. Two-dimensional 𝑐-extremization extremizes a quadratic
anomaly Tr[𝛾3𝑅2] whose Hessian (4.81) is weighted by 𝛾3: right-moving flavors give positive
curvature, left-moving negative, so the metric is indefinite. The same chirality that made two
dimensions dangerous turns the maximization of four dimensions into an extremization here, and
one must solve 𝑘𝑅𝐹= 0 rather than search for the largest 𝑐𝑅.

4.8 N = (0, 2) triality
Three different 2𝑑N = (0, 2) gauge theories can flow to the same infrared N = (0, 2)
superconformal field theory. This is the two-dimensional cousin of Seiberg duality, but with a
decisive difference: it is an order-three relation, a triality, not an order-two involution. In the
graded-quiver ladder the N = (0, 2) brick is the 𝑚= 2 rung, and the duality move is the 𝑛-ality
of order 𝑚+ 1 = 3. We pin the Gadde–Gukov–Putrov conventions exactly, because the flavor
labels are convention-sensitive and the field-theory content is more than a rank map.
The Gadde–Gukov–Putrov node in GGP conventions. The family that carries the statement is

Table 4.2: One N = (0, 2) triality orbit in Gadde–Gukov–Putrov conventions. Each step applies the rank
map 𝑁𝑐↦→𝑁2 −𝑁𝑐together with the flavor cyclic permutation (𝑁1, 𝑁2, 𝑁3) ↦→(𝑁2, 𝑁3, 𝑁1),
and generates the dual matter (permuted quark blocks plus mesons and singlet Fermis). The
intermediate ranks 2, 1, 3 differ and the third step returns the original data (𝑇3 = 1): the orbit
has length three, not two.
Common misconception: N = (0, 2) triality is a 2𝑑Seiberg duality
Seiberg duality in four dimensions is an order-two relation: dualizing twice returns the original theory.
N = (0, 2) triality is order three. The rank map 𝑁𝑐↦→𝑁2 −𝑁𝑐alone is an involution, but the triality
is the rank map together with the flavor cyclic permutation (𝑁1, 𝑁2, 𝑁3) ↦→(𝑁2, 𝑁3, 𝑁1), and the
combined move closes only after three applications, 𝑇3 = 1. The three frames are three different
ultraviolet gauge theories, with dual matter (permuted quarks plus mesons and singlet Fermis), that
flow to the same infrared physics, and the relation is checked by the anomaly, central-charge, and
elliptic-genus matches across all three.
Block C. The shared protected interface

The last two sections are the genuinely shared technology. The elliptic genus (§4.9) is a N = (0, 2)
object that specializes to N = (2, 2) and is the sharpest protected check for both worlds, including
the triality of Block B and the GLSM phases of Block A. A final comparison table (§4.10) reads
the two worlds side by side.
4.9 The elliptic genus
The sharpest protected observable of a 2𝑑N = (0, 2) theory is its elliptic genus, the supersymmet-
ric partition function on a two-torus. It is the two-dimensional analog of the 4𝑑superconformal
index of Section 3: a flavored, signed counting function, invariant under continuous deformations,
that matches across dualities. It is a N = (0, 2) object that specializes to N = (2, 2), which is
why it is the shared tool that closes the section rather than belonging only to Block A or Block B.
A protected index. The elliptic genus is the torus trace, in the Ramond–Ramond sector, of
(−1)𝐹weighted by the left- and right-moving Hamiltonians and the flavor fugacities,
𝑍𝑇2(𝜏, 𝑧, . . . ) = Tr𝑅𝑅

𝑞= 𝑒2𝜋𝑖𝜏, 𝑦= 𝑒2𝜋𝑖𝑧.
(4.101)
Why is it protected? It counts only states annihilated by one right-moving supercharge, with
signs. The right-moving oscillators come in boson-fermion pairs that cancel in the graded trace,
so every state with 𝐻𝑅> 0 drops out and the result is holomorphic in 𝜏(meromorphic in 𝑧),
depending on 𝜏and 𝑧but not ¯𝜏. That cancellation is why the genus is an index, not a full partition
function. For a N = (0, 2) SCFT it is a weak Jacobi form in (𝜏, 𝑧) whose modular weight and
fugacity index are fixed by the anomaly data. It is RG-invariant, and one of the quantities that
must agree across the three triality frames.
Place it among the indices already known. The Witten index Tr(−1)𝐹counts ground
states with signs, one number. The elliptic genus refines it: it keeps the dependence on 𝜏
(through 𝑞𝐻𝐿) and on the fugacities 𝑧, so it is a whole function, and it counts the entire tower of
right-moving-supersymmetric states graded by left-moving energy and flavor charge. Its two
limits recover the coarser indices,

the first keeping the right-moving ground states while retaining the flavor grading, the second
forgetting that grading and reducing to one signed count. At generic 𝑞the elliptic genus is richer
than either limit. Because it is a weak Jacobi form in 𝜏and 𝑧, matching it across a duality is far
stronger than matching one number: two theories can share a Witten index yet differ in genus,
and equality of the genus pins the full protected spectrum. This is why it is the sharpest triality
test in §4.8.

Modularity is gated by the gauge anomaly. The genus is modular only on a consistent theory.
Under 𝜏→−1/𝜏the 𝜃1 and 𝜂pick up known factors that combine into a weak-Jacobi-form
transformation only if the anomalous phases cancel, precisely when the gauge anomaly (4.70)
vanishes,

On the C4 brick (4 −3 −1 = 0) the genus is a weak Jacobi form of fixed weight and index; on a
gauge-anomalous combination (5 −3 −1 = 1 ≠0) the 𝜃1 factors do not close under modular
transformations and there is no modular genus. So an anomaly-free spectrum is a prerequisite for
the genus to be a triality invariant.

recapitulates the structures of Blocks A and B.
Stated and cited, not proved here. The 𝑐-extremization theorem, the 2𝑑c-theorem, the accidental-
symmetry / unitarity-bound corrections, and the proof of N = (0, 2) triality beyond the necessary
anomaly / index matches; the gauged-linear-sigma-model phase theorem (the full quantum-Kähler-moduli
97


## Page 98


/ secondary-fan / discriminant analysis), the elliptic-genus localization / Jeffrey–Kirwan / modularity /
duality machinery, and the A/B-twist topological-correlator / mirror-theorem machinery. All are stated
and, where a computation is possible at toolkit depth, run on fixtures; their proofs are left to the cited
literature.
Further reading
The gauged linear sigma model and its phases are developed in Witten [Wit93]; the original
geometric mirror statement is Greene and Plesser [GP90], and quantum cohomology of toric
targets is in Morrison and Plesser [MP95]. The 𝑡𝑡∗geometry and the classification of two-
dimensional N = 2 theories appear in Cecotti and Vafa [CV91] and Vafa [Vaf91], and the
Bethe/gauge correspondence behind the vacuum equations in Nekrasov and Shatashvili [NS09b;
NS09a]. For (0, 2) models see Silverstein and Witten [SW94]; the two-dimensional conformal
minimal models are in Friedan, Qiu, and Shenker [FQS84], with the standard CFT reference
Di Francesco, Mathieu, and Senechal [DMS97].

5 4𝑑N = 2 supersymmetric field theories
Guide to this section. Sections 1 and 2 fixed the algebra and the common words, and Section 3
built the four-dimensional N = 1 world. This section builds the four-dimensional N = 2 world:
eight real supercharges, the Coulomb-branch special geometry, and Seiberg–Witten theory. It is
a foundations section. It states the special structures of 4𝑑N = 2 field theory and cites the deep
proofs without reproducing them. It writes everything in the N = 1 language of Section 3, so the
multiplet, chiral-ring, R-symmetry, and anomaly machinery is recalled, not re-derived. By the
end you can read an N = 2 theory in N = 1 language, separate its Coulomb (special-Kähler)
branch from its Higgs (hyperkähler) branch, write the Seiberg–Witten periods and the central
charge 𝑍= 𝑎𝑛𝑒+ 𝑎𝐷𝑛𝑚with 𝑀=
√
2 |𝑍|, and locate a theory’s massless BPS points from

5.1 N = 2 multiplets in N = 1 language
A four-dimensional N = 2 theory carries eight real supercharges. Section 1 records this count
as the 8𝑄row of the dimensional ladder, the same amount as 5𝑑N = 1 and 3𝑑N = 4. Eight
supercharges are twice the four of the 4𝑑N = 1 theories of Section 3, so every N = 2 multiplet
splits into a pair of N = 1 multiplets under the N = 1 subalgebra. This is the working description
used throughout the section. It is a chart on the local grammar, not a claim that the second
supersymmetry has gone away.
There are two basic multiplets. The N = 2 vector multiplet decomposes, under the N = 1
subalgebra, into an N = 1 vector multiplet 𝑉and an N = 1 adjoint chiral multiplet Φ,
N = 2 vector =

the gaugino of 𝑉together with the fermion of Φ. The two gauginos are not independent under the
second supersymmetry. They form a doublet of the 𝑆𝑈(2)𝑅that rotates them into each other. The
N = 2 hypermultiplet decomposes into two N = 1 chiral multiplets in conjugate representations,

and dimension of the R-group are all read off the Section 1 ladder.
An N = 2 gauge theory is then built from these pieces: the vector multiplets gauge a group
𝐺, the hypermultiplets sit in a representation of 𝐺, and the second supersymmetry fixes the
superpotential and gauge kinetic term in terms of N = 1 data. The new physics is not the
field content but the eight-supercharge structure the N = 1 language misses: the rigid special
geometry of the Coulomb branch, the curve that solves it, and the BPS central charge.
The canonical N = 2 coupling, in N = 1 words. The pairing rule also pins down the

is what makes N = 2 theories solvable.
5.2 Electromagnetic duality and the charge lattice
On a generic vacuum the adjoint scalar 𝜙acquires a VEV, the gauge group breaks to its Cartan
𝑈(1)𝑟, and the low-energy theory is abelian. An abelian gauge theory in four dimensions carries
both electric and magnetic charges and has an electromagnetic-duality symmetry that exchanges
them. This is the structure the rest of the section solves.
The low-energy coupling is packaged into one complex number, the complexified coupling

The magnetically charged states are solitons: on the Coulomb branch the broken gauge
symmetry supports a ’t Hooft–Polyakov monopole, a finite-energy solution of one unit of magnetic
charge, while a purely electric state is a quantum of the unbroken 𝑈(1). The duality 𝑆exchanges
these two kinds of object. That a soliton and an elementary excitation can be exchanged by a
symmetry is the deep content of electromagnetic duality, and the reason a strong-coupling N = 2
description can look unlike its weak-coupling Lagrangian.

At rank one a 2 × 2 integer matrix preserves 𝐽exactly when its determinant is one, so the

duality group of a rank-𝑟theory is 𝑆𝑝(2𝑟, Z), and 𝑆𝐿(2, Z) is its rank-one face.
Three dualities that wear the same symbol
The letter “S-duality” and the group 𝑆𝐿(2, Z) appear in three distinct places in this section, and
conflating them is the standing error of the section.
1. Low-energy electromagnetic duality on the abelian Coulomb branch (§5.2): the group
𝑆𝑝(2𝑟, Z) acting on the lattice Γem and the period vector Π, 𝑆𝐿(2, Z) at rank one.
2. Monodromy of the period vector around singular loci of the Coulomb branch (§5.4): a
specific subgroup of the low-energy duality group, generated by the degenerations of the
Seiberg–Witten geometry (for pure 𝑆𝑈(2), the 𝑀𝑚, 𝑀𝑑, 𝑀∞below).
3. Exact S-duality of a full superconformal theory (§5.7): a duality of the interacting theory at its
marginal coupling, e.g. 𝑆𝑈(2) with 𝑁𝑓= 4, carrying extra flavor data (Spin(8) triality) and
global-form caveats.
The first is a property of the Cartan theory, the second is a property of the curve, and the third is a
property of the SCFT. They are related but not the same.
This layer comes first because it gives the curve’s monodromies a home: the Seiberg–Witten
solution attaches to each vacuum a coupling 𝜏and a charge vector, and carrying the vacuum
around a singular point acts on the pair by an 𝑆𝑝(2𝑟, Z) matrix (at rank one, 𝑆𝐿(2, Z)). Those
monodromy matrices are exactly the electromagnetic-duality transformations of this section.
5.3 The Coulomb branch and rigid special geometry
The Coulomb branch is the family of vacua on which the adjoint scalars are turned on. Their
VEVs break the gauge group to its Cartan 𝑈(1)𝑟, so the branch has complex dimension the rank,
dimC M𝐶= 𝑟.
(5.17)
It is parametrized not by the gauge-dependent bare VEVs but by the gauge-invariant Casimirs
built from 𝜙, the special coordinates of the abelian theory. For 𝑆𝑈(2) there is one such invariant,
𝑢= ⟨Tr 𝜙2⟩,
(5.18)
and the Coulomb branch is the complex 𝑢-plane. Calling it “the space of scalar VEVs” is
imprecise: the bare VEV ⟨𝜙⟩is gauge-dependent, while the branch is coordinatized by the
gauge-invariant Casimirs and, locally, by the special coordinates 𝑎, 𝑎𝐷the prepotential below
builds. The Coulomb branch is not a generic space of scalar expectation values; it carries a rigid
local special geometry, the data Seiberg–Witten theory solves.
The low-energy effective Lagrangian on the Coulomb branch is fixed by a single holomorphic
function, the prepotential F (𝑎), where 𝑎𝐼are the special coordinates of the 𝑟abelian vector

a frame-dependent potential. From here on, rank one.

5.4 Seiberg–Witten theory and the central charge
The prepotential is holomorphic but not single-valued: around the singular points of the Coulomb
branch it has the 𝑆𝐿(2, Z) monodromies of §5.2. The Seiberg–Witten solution encodes the exact,
non-perturbative F in geometry. Over each point 𝑢it places an auxiliary Riemann surface Σ𝑢,
the Seiberg–Witten curve, carrying a meromorphic one-form 𝜆SW, the Seiberg–Witten differential.
The special coordinate and its dual are the periods of 𝜆SW over a symplectic basis (𝐴, 𝐵) of
one-cycles,

The scale Λ. The scale Λ in the curve (5.29) is not put in by hand. It is the N = 2 dynamical
scale, generated by dimensional transmutation from the asymptotic freedom of the N = 2 gauge
coupling, just as ΛQCD is. The connection to the N = 1 condensate appears only after the
deformation, through scale matching; the condensate is not the origin of Λ.
The matching is one line of holomorphy. Give the adjoint chiral a mass 𝑚and integrate it

5.5 Matter, decoupling, and BPS wall crossing
Pure 𝑆𝑈(2) is the cleanest case but not the generic one. Adding 𝑁𝑓hypermultiplets in the
fundamental keeps the theory N = 2 and enriches both its renormalization-group behavior and
its BPS spectrum. This section adds the matter central charge, the flavor decoupling relation,
the 𝑆𝑈(2) window, and the wall-crossing discipline that Section 1 demanded the N = 2 section
operate.

Crossing the wall, the bound state ceases to exist and its constituents survive. The clean distinction
is between the protected mass formula, the same in every chamber, and the chamber-dependent
spectrum. Pure 𝑆𝑈(2) shows both extremes,

At weak coupling (large 𝑢) the W-boson and the whole dyon tower survive; at strong coupling only
the monopole and dyon that go massless at 𝑢= ±Λ2 remain, the tower having decayed across the
intervening wall. The systematic jump, the Kontsevich–Soibelman formula and spectral-network
technology, is cited but not developed here.
5.6 The hyperkähler Higgs branch
The second branch of an N = 2 moduli space is the Higgs branch, parametrized by the
hypermultiplet expectation values. With eight supercharges this branch is not merely complex. It
is hyperkähler: it carries three complex structures, and the 𝑆𝑈(2)𝑅of §5.1 rotates them into one
another. This is a strictly stronger geometry than the special-Kähler structure of the Coulomb
branch, and the two are different objects with different protections. The Higgs branch does not
run, has no monodromies, and does not receive the special-geometry data of the Coulomb branch.
The Coulomb branch is not hyperkähler. Confusing the two geometries is the second standing
error of the section.
The two branches generically meet on mixed branches, where both vector-multiplet scalars
and hypermultiplet scalars acquire expectation values. The branch nomenclature is Section 2’s:
Coulomb, Higgs, and mixed are frame-dependent labels read from which multiplets carry the
local coordinates.
A worked hyperkähler quotient: 𝑈(1) with 𝑁hypers. One honest dimension computation
keeps this section from being purely Coulomb-branch material. Take the simplest N = 2 gauge
theory with a Higgs branch: a 𝑈(1) vector multiplet with 𝑁hypermultiplets of unit charge.

theory is the standard higher-rank version of exactly this quotient.
5.7 S-duality and the marginal coupling
Some four-dimensional N = 2 theories have an exactly marginal complexified coupling 𝜏, a
coupling whose beta function vanishes so that no scale is generated. The strong- and weak-
coupling regimes of such a 𝜏are physically equivalent, related by an S-duality group of the
113


## Page 114


four-dimensional theory. The structure generalizes the abelian electromagnetic duality of §5.2
from the low-energy Cartan theory to the full interacting theory.
The canonical example is 𝑆𝑈(2) with 𝑁𝑓= 4, the bottom row of Table 5.3. Why this theory
is marginal is the one-line beta-function check,

Argyres–Seiberg duality is a further four-dimensional N = 2 S-duality, relating two different
superconformal descriptions of the same theory, in which a weakly gauged 𝑆𝑈(2) acts on a
strongly interacting sector. We state these as four-dimensional facts and use them as such.
There is a systematic organization of these four-dimensional N = 2 S-dualities, together
with its higher-dimensional origin and the geometric construction of the curves: the class S
construction. This section develops none of it. It states only the purely four-dimensional S-duality
facts, the marginal 𝑁𝑓= 4 coupling and Argyres–Seiberg duality, and cites the class S reference.

5.9 Argyres–Douglas fixed points
A sharper phenomenon occurs at special points of the Coulomb branch. The pure 𝑆𝑈(2) solution
had its two massless states, the monopole (0, 1) and the dyon (−1, 1), at different points of
the 𝑢-plane. In richer theories two BPS states with charges (𝑛𝑒, 𝑛𝑚) and (𝑛′
𝑒, 𝑛′
𝑚) can become
massless at the same point, and if their Dirac pairing is nonzero,
𝑛𝑒𝑛′
𝑚−𝑛𝑚𝑛′
𝑒≠0,
(5.79)
they are mutually non-local. A monopole and a dyon with nonzero pairing are the canonical
example. When two mutually non-local states are simultaneously massless, no 𝑆𝐿(2, Z) duality
frame can make both of them local at once. There is therefore no weakly coupled gauge Lagrangian
at that point. The infrared theory is an interacting, non-Lagrangian N = 2 superconformal field
theory, the Argyres–Douglas theory.
This is the sharpest instance of a Section 2 fact: not every supersymmetric field theory has
a conventional Lagrangian. An Argyres–Douglas fixed point is a genuine field theory, with
well-defined Coulomb-branch and Seiberg–Witten data, flavor symmetries, and central charges;
it simply has no weakly coupled gauge description with elementary fields.
A scaling fixture: fractional Coulomb-branch dimension. The qualitative mechanism deserves
one explicit computation, and the cleanest one is a scaling analysis of the local curve. Near an
116


## Page 117


Argyres–Douglas point the Seiberg–Witten geometry collapses to a canonical local form,
𝑥2 = 𝑧3 + 𝑢,
𝜆SW = 𝑥𝑑𝑧,
(5.80)
where 𝑢is the relevant deformation and 𝜆SW is the Seiberg–Witten differential. The dimensions
of the geometric variables are fixed by two inputs. First, the Seiberg–Witten differential has mass
dimension one, because its period 𝑎=
∮
𝜆SW is the special coordinate and carries dimension of
mass, so
[𝜆SW] = [𝑥] + [𝑧] = 1.
(5.81)
Second, the curve 𝑥2 = 𝑧3 must be scale-covariant, which forces
2[𝑥] = 3[𝑧].
(5.82)
Solving (5.81)–(5.82) gives
[𝑧] = 2
5,
[𝑥] = 3
5,
(5.83)
and the dimension of the Coulomb-branch operator 𝑢follows from [𝑢] = [𝑥2] = 2[𝑥] (equivalently
[𝑢] = [𝑧3] = 3[𝑧], consistently),
Δ(𝑢) = 2[𝑥] = 6
5.
(5.84)
The number is the point. A Coulomb-branch dimension of 6/5 is fractional. A free or weakly
coupled gauge theory has integer Coulomb dimensions,
Δ(Tr 𝜙𝑘) = 𝑘∈Z,
(5.85)
so a fractional dimension is the signature of a genuinely interacting, non-Lagrangian fixed point.
The check code solves (5.81)–(5.84) and confirms Δ(𝑢) = 6/5 is non-integer, and it rejects the
wrong scaling assignment [𝜆SW] = [𝑧] that would give the integer Δ = 3. This fixture is purely
four-dimensional: it uses only the local curve and its differential, with no higher-dimensional
construction.
For this simplest (rank-one) Argyres–Douglas point the section can pin only its rank one, its
computed dimension Δ(𝑢) = 6/5, the absence of a weakly coupled Lagrangian, and the mutual
non-locality of its light states. It was identified in Argyres and Douglas [AD95]; its central
charges 𝑎and 𝑐are fixed in the literature by later work, so we route their determination onward
rather than invent numbers in prose.
117


## Page 118


An N = 2 SCFT data card
A non-Lagrangian N = 2 superconformal theory has no weakly coupled fields to list, but it still
carries a well-defined set of protected data. The disciplined way to read such a theory is to record:
• the Coulomb-branch operator dimensions Δ𝑖(e.g. Δ(𝑢) = 6/5 for the fixture above), which
need not be integers;
• the Higgs branch as a hyperkähler variety and the global (flavor) symmetry;
• the flavor central charges 𝑘𝐹, the coefficient of the flavor-current two-point function;
• the conformal central charges 𝑎and 𝑐;
• the BPS charge lattice Γem with its Dirac pairing (5.12) and any defect/line-operator data;
• the Seiberg–Witten curve and differential (5.80), or a class S construction, when one is
available.
This card is the interface-level reading; the systematic determination of 𝑎, 𝑐, and 𝑘𝐹for non-Lagrangian
theories is cited, not computed here.
The mechanism, two mutually non-local BPS states massless together, the fractional Coulomb-
branch dimension, and the non-Lagrangian conclusion are stated and worked here; the systematic
construction of these theories is cited rather than developed here.

massless together; no duality frame makes both local, so the IR is an interacting non-Lagrangian N = 2

fractional Δ(𝑢) = 6/5 (5.84); the N = 2 SCFT data card. (Argyres and Douglas [AD95]).
Further reading
The Seiberg–Witten solution has the pedagogical review Lerche [Ler97] and the lecture notes
Tachikawa [Tac14]. Instanton counting and the prepotential are in Nekrasov and Okounkov
[NO06], extended to quivers in Nekrasov and Pestun [NP23]; localization on 𝑆4 in Pestun [Pes12]
with the review Pestun et al. [Pes+17]; the AGT correspondence in Alday, Gaiotto, and Tachikawa
[AGT10]. BPS spectra and wall-crossing appear in Gaiotto, Moore, and Neitzke [GMN13;
GMN12]. Argyres–Douglas and other non-Lagrangian fixed points are in Argyres, Plesser,
Seiberg, and Witten [APSW96] and Xie [Xie13], their central charges in Shapere and Tachikawa
[ST08], the geometric constraints on Coulomb branches in Argyres, Lotito, Lü, and Martone
[ALLM18], and the associated chiral algebra in Beem, Lemos, Liendo, Peelaers, Rastelli, and
Rees [BLLPRR15].

6 3𝑑supersymmetric field theories
Guide to this section. Sections 1 and 2 fixed the algebra and the common words, and Sections 3
and 5 built the four-dimensional N = 1 and N = 2 worlds. This section builds the three-
dimensional world. Three dimensions is where two pieces of physics appear that the higher-
dimensional sections do not have. First, the photon is dual to a compact scalar, the dual photon,
so the Coulomb branch is a quantum object built by monopole operators rather than a classical
scalar-VEV space. Second, the exact superconformal R-symmetry is fixed by F-maximization
of the round-sphere free energy, the 3𝑑member of the extremization family whose prototype
(𝑎-maximization) Section 3 owns. It is a foundations section, but a working one. It states the
field-theory facts and then runs them as computations: the dual photon and the topological
𝑈(1)𝐽; the Borokhov–Kapustin–Wu dimension of a monopole operator; F-maximization to the
superconformal R; Chern-Simons-matter theories, the parity anomaly, and ABJM with its 𝑁3/2
free energy; the twin hyperkähler branches of N = 4; and the sharpest duality in these notes,
three-dimensional mirror symmetry, which exchanges the Coulomb and Higgs branches. The
deep theorems (F-maximization, the 𝐹-theorem, mirror symmetry, and the 3𝑑dualities) are
stated and cited rather than proved. By the end you can take a 3𝑑supersymmetric theory, given
by a Lagrangian or a quiver, identify its multiplets and branches, write its monopole operators
and their dimensions, run F-maximization, read off its Chern-Simons levels and parity-anomaly
condition, and use mirror symmetry to compute the hard branch from the easy one.
122


## Page 123


What this section delivers
The 3𝑑N = 2 multiplets, the real scalar 𝜎in the vector multiplet, and the dualization of the

6.1 N = 2 multiplets and the dual photon
A 3𝑑N = 2 theory has four real supercharges. This is the same count as 4𝑑N = 1: dimensional
reduction of a four-dimensional N = 1 theory on a circle gives a three-dimensional N = 2 theory,
so the two share their supercharge algebra and their R-symmetry 𝑈(1)𝑅. The ladder table of
Section 1 records this as the 4𝑄row 4𝑑N = 1 = 3𝑑N = 2 = 2𝑑(2, 2). Four real supercharges
is twice the minimal three-dimensional spinor: a minimal 3𝑑spinor is two-component real, so
3𝑑N = 1 has two supercharges and 3𝑑N = 2 has 2×2 = 4, the four-supercharge workhorse. We
take the count and the multiplet grammar as given and build the genuinely new three-dimensional
structure on top of it.
The R-symmetry is a single abelian 𝑈(1)𝑅, inherited from the four-dimensional 𝑈(1)𝑅.
As in four dimensions, the fermion in a chiral multiplet has R-charge one less than the scalar,
𝑅𝜓= Δ −1 (with Δ the scalar R-charge), and the gaugino has R-charge +1. This shift is the
input to the monopole dimension (6.18) and to F-maximization (§6.4); we recall it, and do not
re-derive the multiplet R-charges, which are Section 2 grammar.
The two multiplets are the ones fixed in Section 2, read in three dimensions.
• Chiral Φ: a complex scalar 𝜙, a Dirac fermion 𝜓, and an auxiliary field 𝐹.
• Vector 𝑉: a gauge field 𝐴𝜇, a gaugino, an auxiliary field 𝐷, and one extra real scalar that
is the first new fact of the section. Reducing a four-dimensional vector multiplet to three
dimensions, the gauge field 𝐴𝑀, 𝑀= 0, 1, 2, 3, splits into a three-dimensional gauge field
𝐴𝜇, 𝜇= 0, 1, 2, and one leftover component 𝐴3. That leftover has no gauge index to carry
in three dimensions, so it becomes a real scalar 𝜎sitting in the vector multiplet alongside
the photon:

Common misconception: the 3𝑑Coulomb branch is a classical scalar-VEV space
It is tempting to picture the 3𝑑Coulomb branch as the direct analog of the four-dimensional one, a
moduli space of classical adjoint scalar VEVs. It is not. The photon dualizes to a compact scalar,
so the branch is parametrized by 𝜎together with the dual photon 𝛾, and the operators whose VEVs
coordinatize it are monopole operators (§6.3), not polynomials in a classical field; in N = 4 its chiral
ring is monopole-generated and quantum-corrected (§6.8). The adjoint-VEV picture is only the
semiclassical approximation, valid far out on 𝜎where the theory is weakly coupled.

Block A. The 3𝑑N = 2 language and Chern–Simons matter
The two preceding sections are the common three-dimensional gateway: the spinor count, the
real scalar 𝜎, the dual photon, and the topological 𝑈(1)𝐽are shared by every 3𝑑supersymmetric
theory. The next five sections build the four-supercharge N = 2 theory, the lower-supersymmetry
workhorse and the local language in which monopoles, Chern–Simons levels, F-maximization,
and (in Block B) the N = 4 enhancement are all written. We present N = 2 first for that reason,
exactly as 4𝑑N = 1 preceded N = 2 and 2𝑑(2, 2) preceded (0, 2); ABJM is included here
because it is written in N = 2 superspace even though its supersymmetry enhances. They
are monopole operators and the Borokhov–Kapustin–Wu dimension (§6.3), F-maximization
to the superconformal R (§6.4), Chern-Simons-matter and the parity anomaly (§6.5), ABJM
with its 𝑁3/2 free energy (§6.6), and the Aharony and Giveon–Kutasov dualities (§6.7). The
eight-supercharge N = 4 world, where mirror symmetry lives, follows as Block B.
6.3 Monopole operators and their dimensions
A 3𝑑monopole operator is a local operator, but not one built from the elementary fields. It is a
disorder operator: to insert it at a point 𝑥, one declares that the path integral is taken over field
configurations with a prescribed singularity at 𝑥. On a small two-sphere 𝑆2 surrounding 𝑥, the
gauge field is required to carry the flux of a Dirac monopole of magnetic charge 𝑚,
1

with 𝑚valued in the GNO (Goddard–Nuyts–Olive) lattice, the coweight lattice of the gauge
group in its declared global form. For 𝑈(1) the charge 𝑚is an integer; for a nonabelian group it
is a cocharacter, an embedding 𝑈(1) ↩→𝑇into the maximal torus, taken modulo the Weyl group.
The operator is well defined because the flux (6.16) is conserved: inserting it creates one unit (or
𝑚units) of the 𝑈(1)𝐽charge (6.12), so a bare monopole operator of magnetic charge 𝑚carries
topological charge 𝑄𝐽= 𝑚.
To read off its quantum numbers one uses the state-operator map. In a conformal field theory
a local operator at the origin of R3 corresponds, under the conformal map

in the background of the magnetic flux 𝑚. At the superconformal point the energy equals the
R-charge of the lowest state in the flux sector. The flux deforms the mode expansion of every
charged field: a charged fermion in a monopole background develops Landau levels on 𝑆2, and
its filled Dirac sea carries a net R-charge. Summing these contributions gives the dimension. The
counting is the Borokhov–Kapustin–Wu formula. Written so the matter and the gauge (gaugino)
contributions are kept separate,

and flux attachment is why the Giveon–Kutasov dual (§6.7) has no monopole singlets while the
Aharony dual does.
6.4 F-maximization
When several abelian symmetries can mix into the R-symmetry, the superconformal R is a definite
linear combination, fixed by an extremization principle. In four dimensions it maximizes the
central charge 𝑎(Section 3); in three dimensions it maximizes the real part of the round-sphere
free energy. This is F-maximization (Jafferis), the 3𝑑member of the extremization family whose
prototype Section 3 owns.
The object that is extremized is the free energy of the theory on a round three-sphere.
Supersymmetric localization reduces the 𝑆3 partition function to a finite-dimensional matrix
integral, a function 𝑍[Δ] of the trial R-charges Δ𝑖assigned to the matter. We do not derive the
localization here; we state its output and run it. A chiral multiplet of trial R-charge Δ contributes
a factor to 𝑍whose logarithm is the one-loop function

The spin theories with fermions used below refine this statement: the bare level can be integer or

The Chern-Simons term changes the gauge dynamics in two ways we use. First, it gives
the photon a topological mass ∼𝑘𝑔2, so a pure Chern-Simons gauge field is gapped rather than
dualizing to a massless dual photon; the Coulomb branch is lifted. Second, it attaches flux to
charge. Vary (6.39) for a 𝑈(1) factor together with the matter current: the equation of motion for
the time component 𝐴0 reads

𝑞gauge = 𝑘𝑚,

which is (6.26), now derived from the Gauss law rather than quoted. The Chern-Simons term
thus ties magnetic flux to gauge charge: with 𝑘≠0 a bare monopole is gauge-charged, so it is not
a gauge-invariant operator on its own and must be dressed by matter to become one. Depending
on the global form of the gauge group and the level, the continuous topological symmetry 𝑈(1)𝐽
can then be reduced or mixed with the gauge data rather than remaining an independent global
symmetry. We do not say the Chern-Simons term simply Higgses 𝑈(1)𝐽; the precise statement
is flux attachment and the dressing it forces. This is the origin of the difference between the
Aharony and Giveon–Kutasov dualities in §6.7.
The parity anomaly. A single massless Dirac fermion in three dimensions cannot be quantized
in a way that preserves both gauge invariance and parity. Regulating the fermion determinant
induces a half-integer Chern-Simons term, so a theory with an odd number of charged fermions
requires a half-integer bare level to be consistent. This is the parity anomaly (Redlich). For a
gauge representation 𝑅the admissible bare level satisfies

Common misconception: the ABJM gauge group is 𝑆𝑈(4) with an effective level
Two errors travel together here. First, the N = 6 R-symmetry is 𝑆𝑈(4), and it acts on the matter,
but it is not the gauge group: the ABJM gauge group is 𝑈(𝑁)𝑘× 𝑈(𝑁)−𝑘, with the declared global
quotient as global-form data, and the ABJM matter weights (1, 1, 1, 1)/𝑘lie in 𝑈(4), not 𝑆𝑈(4).
Second, these notes quote the bare level 𝑘. The parity anomaly (6.43) is a quantization condition
on the bare level (the 𝑘+ 𝑁𝑓/2 ∈Z form is the fundamental special case, not the general law), a
constraint, not a redefinition. The global form (the diagonal 𝑈(1), the Z𝑘quotient) affects the line
and monopole spectrum and the C4/Z𝑘orbifold statement; the full line-operator classification is not
developed here.
6.7 The Aharony and Giveon–Kutasov dualities
Three-dimensional N = 2 gauge theories have their own Seiberg-type infrared dualities, and
the monopole operators of §6.3 make them differ from the four-dimensional Seiberg duality
of Section 3 in a way worth stating precisely. There are two, distinguished by whether a
Chern-Simons term is present.
134


## Page 135


Aharony duality (no Chern-Simons term). A 𝑈(𝑁𝑐) theory with 𝑁𝑓flavors (and no Chern-
Simons term) is infrared-dual to a 𝑈(𝑁𝑓−𝑁𝑐) theory with 𝑁𝑓dual flavors, a gauge-singlet
meson 𝑀, and, crucially, two extra gauge-singlet monopole operators 𝑉±, all coupled through a
superpotential. The rank map is
𝑈(𝑁𝑐) ←→𝑈(𝑁𝑓−𝑁𝑐),
(6.54)
and the monopole singlets 𝑉± are the three-dimensional feature with no four-dimensional analog:
the electric theory’s monopole operators are dynamical, so the magnetic dual carries them as
explicit singlet fields. The map is an involution, as any duality must be:

Common misconception: the 3𝑑dualities are the reduction of 4𝑑Seiberg duality
Reducing four-dimensional Seiberg duality on a circle does not give the three-dimensional dualities
on the nose. The reduction-and-flow generates extra monopole superpotential terms, so the Aharony
dual carries explicit monopole singlets 𝑉± that the naive four-dimensional map has no room for; and a
Chern-Simons level changes the rank map entirely, 𝑈(𝑁𝑐)𝑘→𝑈(𝑁𝑓+ |𝑘| −𝑁𝑐)−𝑘, which has no
four-dimensional counterpart. The same warning applies, even more sharply, to mirror symmetry
(§6.9), which has no four-dimensional analog at all.

Block B. The 3𝑑N = 4 world
Doubling to eight supercharges opens the geometric, mirror world of three dimensions. Three-
dimensional N = 4 mirror symmetry was one of the earliest sharp understandings of 3𝑑
supersymmetric dynamics: it exchanges the Higgs and Coulomb branches of a pair of theories,
and thereby makes a quantum, monopole-generated Coulomb branch computable from the
classical Higgs branch of the mirror. Where Block A treats N = 2 as the lower-supersymmetry
workhorse (monopoles, F-maximization, Chern-Simons-matter and ABJM, the Aharony and
Giveon–Kutasov dualities), Block B is the eight-supercharge world: two hyperkähler branches,
the 𝑆𝑈(2)𝐶× 𝑆𝑈(2)𝐻R-symmetry that acts on them separately, and mirror symmetry as a
central part of the subject rather than an appendix. The two sections build the twin branches
(§6.8) and then the mirror exchange that swaps them (§6.9).
6.8 The N = 4 enhancement: two branches
Doubling the supersymmetry to 3𝑑N = 4 gives eight real supercharges, the same count as
4𝑑N = 2: dimensional reduction of a four-dimensional N = 2 theory on a circle gives a
three-dimensional N = 4 theory, the 8𝑄row of the Section 1 ladder table. The enhancement
changes the R-symmetry qualitatively. Where N = 2 has a single 𝑈(1)𝑅, the N = 4 theory has
𝑆𝑈(2)𝐶× 𝑆𝑈(2)𝐻= Spin(4),
(6.63)
two commuting 𝑆𝑈(2) factors (Section 1 master table): so(4) = su(2) ⊕su(2), rank 1 + 1 = 2,
dimension 3 + 3 = 6. The two factors act separately on the two kinds of branch. This is not a

Common misconception: the N = 4 Coulomb branch is special-Kähler
The four-dimensional N = 2 Coulomb branch is special-Kähler; the three-dimensional N = 4
Coulomb branch is hyperkähler. It is not the four-dimensional special-Kähler object reduced
naively. The dual photon supplies the fourth real coordinate (6.67), promoting the local geometry
to quaternionic, and the branch is quantum-corrected and monopole-built rather than governed by
a classical prepotential. Nor is the R-symmetry a single 𝑆𝑈(2)𝑅: it is 𝑆𝑈(2)𝐶× 𝑆𝑈(2)𝐻, two
factors acting on the two branches separately, and this two-factor structure is what mirror symmetry
exchanges.
Worked instance: the 𝑈(1) Coulomb-branch chiral ring
The monopole operators do more than carry topological charge; in N = 4 they generate the
Coulomb-branch chiral ring. For 𝑈(1) with 𝑁𝑓flavors the ring is generated by the two bare
monopoles 𝑉+ (charge +1) and 𝑉−(charge −1) together with the vector-multiplet complex scalar
Φ (the complexified 𝜎). The product 𝑉+𝑉−has vanishing topological charge, so it is a genuine
function of Φ; its R-charge is fixed by the monopole dimensions. Using the SQED value

6.9 Three-dimensional mirror symmetry
Three-dimensional mirror symmetry is the sharpest duality in these notes. It is a duality of N = 4
theories that exchanges the two branches: for a theory 𝑇and its mirror 𝑇∨,
𝐶(𝑇)  𝐻(𝑇∨),
𝐻(𝑇)  𝐶(𝑇∨),
(6.75)
the Coulomb branch of one theory equal to the Higgs branch of the other and vice versa (the
branch frame is always carried: 𝐶(𝑇∨)  𝐻(𝑇) is a claim, not a renaming). Under the exchange
the two R-symmetry factors swap, 𝑆𝑈(2)𝐶↔𝑆𝑈(2)𝐻, and the exchange dictionary swaps the
data that source the two branches:
FI parameters ←→hypermultiplet masses,
𝑈(1)𝐽←→a flavor symmetry.
(6.76)
Written as a single line, with the branch frame carried and the FI parameter 𝜁𝑇and mass 𝑚𝑇∨
named on the two theories, the mirror dictionary is
Coulomb branch of 𝑇↔Higgs branch of 𝑇∨,
𝜁𝑇↔𝑚𝑇∨,
𝑈(1)𝐽,𝑇↔𝑈(1)𝐹,𝑇∨.
(6.77)
The dictionary is an involution: applying it twice returns each item to itself. Its computational
power is the asymmetry of difficulty between the branches: the Coulomb branch is quantum and
monopole-generated, the Higgs branch a classical hyperkähler quotient, so mirror symmetry
turns a hard monopole computation on one side into an easy polynomial computation on the
other.

independently and only then matching them.

The 3𝑑N = 4 deformation dictionary
The two deformation parameters resolve the two branches, and the assignment is the opposite of the
naive guess. In a 3𝑑N = 4 gauge theory,
FI 𝜁resolves Higgs,
real mass 𝑚resolves Coulomb.
Mirror symmetry exchanges the two deformations along with the two branches:
𝜁(𝑇) ←→𝑚(𝑇∨),
𝐻(𝑇) ←→𝐶(𝑇∨).
So the FI parameter of 𝑇resolves the Higgs branch of 𝑇, which is the Coulomb branch of 𝑇∨, resolved
there by the mirror mass. Do not say the FI parameter resolves the Coulomb branch of the same
theory: it resolves that theory’s Higgs branch (equivalently, the mirror’s Coulomb branch).
The exchange dictionary makes the match more than a coincidence of geometries, though
the basic pair realizes it degenerately. Under (6.76) the FI parameter 𝜁of the gauge theory (the

deforms the free hyper’s Higgs branch, the branch mirror to the gauge theory’s Coulomb branch,

Because the free hyper has no nontrivial Coulomb branch, this pair is not a useful example of a

the topological current with no Lagrangian origin becoming an ordinary flavor current with

exchanged branch on the other, which is what promotes the geometric match to a duality.
For the self-mirror 𝑇[𝑆𝑈(2)] the dictionary closes on itself: at 𝑁𝑓= 2 the topological
symmetry enhances, and the exchange of the flavor 𝑆𝑈(2) (Higgs side, rotating the two hypers)
with it,

(6.84)
is a symmetry of the theory itself, the two factors on an equal footing.
Beyond these worked pairs, the systematic construction of mirror pairs, by brane realizations
(the Hanany–Witten setup) and magnetic quivers, is cited but not developed here; the proof of
mirror symmetry is also cited rather than reproduced. This section delivers the statement, the
exchange dictionary, and the two worked pairs.

Sources and notes
Sources and notes. This is the three-dimensional dimension section of these notes, the Coulomb-branch,
more-rigid partner of the 4𝑑N = 2 section.
§6.1 Multiplets and the dual photon. The 3𝑑N = 2 four-supercharge count as the reduction of
4𝑑N = 1, the vector multiplet’s real scalar 𝜎= 𝐴3 (6.1), the dualization of the photon ★𝐹∼𝑑𝛾(6.4), and
the two-real-dimensional rank-1 Coulomb branch. (Aharony, Hanany, Intriligator, Seiberg, and Strassler
[AHISS97] the 3𝑑Coulomb-branch spine).

Stated, not proved here. F-maximization, the 𝐹-theorem, and the 𝑆3 localization derivation of 𝑍[Δ];
the proof of three-dimensional mirror symmetry, the systematic N = 4 Coulomb-branch geometry
(abelianization, Hilbert series, magnetic quivers), the monopole-operator conformal-dimension derivation
by radial quantization, and the proofs of the Aharony / Giveon–Kutasov dualities with the partition-
function-identity machinery; and the gauge global-form / GNO-coweight lattice classification. All are
stated and run on the SQED / ABJM / mirror fixtures here; their proofs are left to the cited literature.
Further reading
Three-dimensional mirror symmetry originates in Intriligator and Seiberg [IS96] and Boer, Hori,
Ooguri, and Oz [BHOO97], with the abelian case in Kapustin and Strassler [KS99] and its brane
realization in Hanany and Witten [HW97]. Monopole operators are quantized in Borokhov,
Kapustin, and Wu [BKW02] and their supersymmetry enhancement in Bashkirov and Kapustin
[BK11]; the Coulomb-branch Hilbert series in Cremonesi, Hanany, and Zaffaroni [CHZ14] and
the full branch geometry in Bullimore, Dimofte, and Gaiotto [BDG17]. 𝐹-maximization is treated
in Jafferis [Jaf12] and Closset, Dumitrescu, Festuccia, Komargodski, and Seiberg [CDFKS12].
The ABJM theory appears in Aharony, Bergman, Jafferis, and Maldacena [ABJM08], with the
Bagger–Lambert–Gustavsson construction Bagger and Lambert [BL07] and Gustavsson [Gus09]
and the boundary theory 𝑇[𝑆𝑈(𝑁)] in Gaiotto and Witten [GW09]. Three-dimensional dualities
are surveyed in Aharony, Razamat, Seiberg, and Willett [ARSW13], and supersymmetric partition
functions and indices in Benini and Zaffaroni [BZ15], Closset and Kim [CK16], Dimofte, Gaiotto,
and Gukov [DGG14], and Gaiotto and Koroteev [GK13].

7 5𝑑supersymmetric field theories
Guide to this section. Sections 1 and 2 fixed the algebra and the common words, and Section 5
built the four-dimensional world of eight supercharges, 4𝑑N = 2 field theory, with its complex
Coulomb branch and its holomorphic special geometry. This section raises the same eight
supercharges to five dimensions. Almost everything changes shape. The gauge coupling is now
146


## Page 147


irrelevant, so the Yang–Mills theory is not a fundamental description but the deformation of
something above it; the Coulomb branch is real rather than complex; the low-energy data on it
is a real cubic prepotential rather than a holomorphic one; and the ultraviolet completion is an
intrinsically strongly-coupled superconformal field theory with an exceptional flavor symmetry
that no Lagrangian makes manifest. It is a foundations section, but a working one. It states
the five-dimensional facts and then runs them as computations: the multiplet content and
the sign flip in the coupling, the real Coulomb branch and its Weyl-chamber walls, the cubic
prepotential assembled from one-loop |𝑚|3 contributions and differentiated to its metric, the
instanton operators and their topological current, the 𝐸𝑁𝑓+1 flavor enhancement counted two
independent ways, the 5𝑑superconformal fixed points, and the superconformal index that makes
the enhancement visible. Only the deep theorems (the existence and classification of the 5𝑑
superconformal theories, the full instanton partition function) are stated and cited rather than
proved. By the end you can take a 5𝑑N = 1 gauge theory, identify its real Coulomb branch,
assemble its cubic prepotential and read its metric as a second derivative, define its instanton
operators and count the 𝐸𝑁𝑓+1 flavor enhancement, and treat the inverse coupling 1/𝑔2
0 as a
dimensionful mass parameter rather than a marginal coupling.
What this section delivers
The 5𝑑N = 1 vector and hypermultiplet, and the irrelevant coupling [𝑔2] = −1 that makes 1/𝑔2
0 a
mass parameter (§7.1); the real Coulomb branch as a Weyl chamber, contrasted with the complex
special-Kähler branch of 4𝑑N = 2 (§7.2); the real cubic prepotential F (𝜙), classical plus one-loop
exact, with the instanton data held separate, and its second-derivative metric worked on 𝑆𝑈(2) + 𝑁𝑓
to the leading cubic coefficient 8 −𝑁𝑓(§7.3); instanton operators, the topological 𝑈(1)𝐼current
𝑗𝐼=

8𝜋2 Tr(𝐹∧𝐹), and the 𝐸𝑁𝑓+1 flavor enhancement counted through the 𝑁𝑓= 5 →𝐸6 branching
78 = 45 + 1 + 16 + 16 (§7.4); the 5𝑑superconformal fixed points, the no-Lagrangian fact, and

7.1 Multiplets and the irrelevant coupling
A 5𝑑N = 1 theory carries eight real supercharges. The Section 1 ladder fixes the count from the
minimal five-dimensional spinor and reads off the R-symmetry in one line,
#Q = (min 5𝑑spinor) × N = 8 × 1 = 8,
𝑅= 𝑆𝑈(2)𝑅= Spin(3),
dim 𝑆𝑈(2)𝑅= 3.
(7.1)
The same eight land on 4𝑑N = 2 (Section 5), 6𝑑(1, 0) (next section), and 3𝑑N = 4 (Section 6):
5𝑑N = 1 = 4𝑑N = 2 = 6𝑑(1, 0) = 3𝑑N = 4 = 8 Q.
(7.2)
We do not re-derive (7.1); we read it off the master table and build on it.
Two multiplets carry the supersymmetry. The vector multiplet is a gauge field 𝐴𝜇, one real
adjoint scalar 𝜙, and a symplectic-Majorana gaugino in the 𝑆𝑈(2)𝑅doublet,

Because (7.10) makes 1/𝑔2
0 a mass, it is precisely the real mass of the topological 𝑈(1)𝐼
symmetry of §7.4: turning it on is a relevant deformation that drives the theory off the ultraviolet
fixed point down onto the Coulomb branch. It is emphatically not a Coulomb-branch modulus;
the branch is parametrized by 𝜙, while 1/𝑔2
0 is a fixed external parameter, the coefficient of the
quadratic term in the prepotential of §7.3. There is no 5𝑑holomorphic coupling 𝜏: the classical
data is the single real number 1/𝑔2
0, and the branch dependence is carried entirely by 𝜙.
7.2 The real Coulomb branch
Give the real adjoint scalar 𝜙of the vector multiplet a vacuum expectation value in the Cartan
subalgebra of the gauge group 𝐺. Generically this breaks 𝐺to its maximal torus,
𝐺−→𝑈(1)𝑟,
𝑟= rank 𝐺,
(7.11)
and the low-energy theory is 𝑟free abelian vector multiplets. The moduli space of these vacua is
the Coulomb branch, parametrized by the 𝑟real Cartan components 𝜙𝑖modulo the residual Weyl
action. It is a real cone of dimension 𝑟, a Weyl chamber,
dimR MCoulomb = rank 𝐺.
(7.12)
A charged state of weight 𝑤acquires mass |𝑤· 𝜙| on the branch; the W-bosons carry the roots, so
their mass is |𝛼· 𝜙|, vanishing on a root wall where the gauge symmetry re-enhances.
For 𝑆𝑈(2) the Cartan is one-dimensional, the Weyl group is Z2 acting by 𝜙↦→−𝜙, and the
chamber is the half-line
𝜙≥0,

one ray with a single wall at the origin. At 𝜙= 0 the W-bosons become massless and the full
𝑆𝑈(2) is restored; for 𝜙> 0 the theory is a free 𝑈(1) with massive charged states. This half-line
is the arena for every 𝑆𝑈(2) computation in the section.

which does not restore gauge symmetry but does change the massive spectrum being integrated
out, so the low-energy prepotential of §7.3 is a different cubic on either side. The branch is tiled
into sub-chambers by the root and weight walls together, and F is piecewise cubic.

The real dimension (7.12) is the second structural break from Section 5. The 4𝑑N = 2
branch is complex of complex dimension 𝑟, real dimension 2𝑟, carrying the special-Kähler
geometry of the Seiberg–Witten curve. The 5𝑑branch is real, dimension 𝑟; the missing factor of
two is exactly the circle holonomy of (7.7),
dimR M5𝑑= 𝑟
×𝑆1, 𝜙→𝜙+𝑖𝐴5
−−−−−−−−−−−−−−→dimC M4𝑑= 𝑟,
(7.17)
so a 5𝑑real branch of dimension 𝑟complexifies to a 4𝑑complex branch of complex dimension 𝑟.
In five dimensions itself the branch is real, and its low-energy data is the real cubic prepotential
of §7.3, not a holomorphic special geometry.
Common misconception: the 5𝑑Coulomb branch is the complex special-Kähler branch of
4𝑑N = 2
It is tempting to carry over the 4𝑑N = 2 picture wholesale: a complex Coulomb branch with a
holomorphic prepotential, a Seiberg–Witten curve, and a marginal gauge coupling 𝜏. All three fail in
five dimensions. The 5𝑑N = 1 Coulomb branch is real, parametrized by the single real adjoint scalar
𝜙of each Cartan vector multiplet; it is a real cone, a Weyl chamber, not the complex special-Kähler
manifold of 4𝑑N = 2 (which carries the holomorphic prepotential and the Seiberg–Witten curve of
Section 5). The 5𝑑gauge coupling 𝑔2 has mass dimension −1, so the Yang–Mills term is irrelevant
and 1/𝑔2
0 is a dimensionful mass parameter (the real mass of the topological 𝑈(1)𝐼symmetry, a
relevant deformation of the ultraviolet fixed point, appearing as the bare quadratic piece of the cubic
prepotential), not a marginal coupling and not a Coulomb-branch modulus. The complex special
geometry of four dimensions is recovered only after compactifying on a circle, where the holonomy
𝐴5 complexifies 𝜙.
7.3 The cubic prepotential and its metric
The low-energy effective action on the 5𝑑Coulomb branch is controlled by a single real function
F (𝜙), the prepotential. It plays the same role as the holomorphic prepotential of 4𝑑N = 2
(Section 5), encoding the branch metric as a second derivative, but it is a real cubic rather than
an arbitrary holomorphic function.
Why cubic, and why one-loop exact? The prepotential enters the two-derivative effective
Lagrangian through

## Page 151


Why the cubic is one-loop exact
The cubic form of (7.19) follows from dimensions once the data is written in vector-multiplet variables.
That the cubic receives no corrections beyond one loop is a supersymmetric nonrenormalization
statement, resting on three inputs: eight supercharges constrain the two-derivative vector-multiplet
action to the special-real form F ; locality plus the quantization of the 5𝑑Chern–Simons level
forbid a continuous renormalization of F𝑖𝑗𝑘; and integrating out a massive BPS multiplet shifts the
cubic Chern–Simons coupling only at one loop, by the |𝑚|3 term below. Higher-loop corrections
are excluded from this two-derivative vector-multiplet prepotential, and higher-derivative terms
belong to separate effective couplings, not to this local cubic. The instanton data of §7.4 is separate
protected/index data, not a local polynomial tail: it is never a third additive piece of this cubic.
The one-loop shift is fixed by the same counting. A massive multiplet of mass 𝑚contributes
a dimension-three, parity-even term built from 𝑚, hence ∝|𝑚|3 (the physical mass is |𝑚|), with a
sign set by statistics,

Common misconception: the 𝐸𝑛enhancement enlarges the R-symmetry
The enhanced exceptional symmetry 𝐸𝑁𝑓+1 at the 5𝑑ultraviolet fixed point is a flavor symmetry, not
an R-symmetry. The 5𝑑N = 1 R-symmetry is 𝑆𝑈(2)𝑅everywhere along the flow and at the fixed
point; the Section 1 master table is explicit on this. The enhancement assembles the perturbative flavor
symmetry 𝑆𝑂(2𝑁𝑓) ×𝑈(1)𝐼, in which the 𝑈(1)𝐼is the topological instanton-number symmetry (also
a flavor symmetry, not R), with the conserved currents carried by the 𝑘= ±1 instanton operators, into
the flavor group 𝐸𝑁𝑓+1. The R-symmetry 𝑆𝑈(2)𝑅, of dimension three, is untouched: dim 𝐸6 = 78 is
a flavor symmetry, not a three-dimensional R-symmetry. Conflating the flavor enhancement with an
enlargement of the R-symmetry is the standard five-dimensional trap the master table warns against.

Common misconception: a 5𝑑theory without a Lagrangian is not a real theory
A 5𝑑gauge theory is non-renormalizable, so its ultraviolet completion is an intrinsically strongly-
coupled superconformal field theory, the 𝐸𝑛fixed point, which has no Lagrangian description; the
gauge theory is a relevant (mass) deformation of the fixed point, not the other way around. These 5𝑑
superconformal theories are genuine quantum field theories. They have well-defined Coulomb-branch
prepotentials, instanton operators, exceptional flavor symmetries, and superconformal indices, and
their observables can be computed, even though no weakly-coupled Lagrangian exists. A missing
Lagrangian is not a missing theory. This continues the non-Lagrangian boundary named in Section 2
and echoes the four-dimensional Argyres–Douglas theories of Section 5: the absence of a Lagrangian
is a feature of many strongly-coupled fixed points, not a defect.
The convexity of the prepotential is the field-theory probe of the fixed point: the metric
positivity (7.35) bounds the allowed matter,
